, offering additional empirical evidence for the asymptotic
normality of
\check{\theta}_{j}(u_{0})
.

Figure 3
:
The histogram of normalized estimators
\check{\theta}_{j}(u_{0})=\tfrac{\hat{\theta}_{\mathrm{TL},j}(u_{0})-\theta_{j}(u_{0})}{\hat{{\sf SE}}\left(\hat{\theta}_{\mathrm{TL},j}(u_{0})\right)}
when
\rho_{n}\to 0
and
\infty
.

4.3

Phase transition in the rate of estimation

In this subsection, we demonstrate the phase transition in the rate of convergence of
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})
. Recall that we established in Theorem
3.6
that

\mathrm{MSE}(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0}))\lesssim n_{0}^{-1}\wedge\max\left\{(K/\gamma)^{-2\beta},\,(n/\gamma)^{-\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}},\,n^{-1}\right\}.

(4.1)

It follows immediately that, depending on the choice of
(K,n,n_{0},\gamma,\beta)
, the rate of
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})
transitions between different regimes. The goal of this subsection is to numerically illustrate this phase transition behavior by varying these parameters. We divide our presentation into three parts, depending on whether we vary
K
,
\gamma
, or
n
. To visualize the convergence rates, we present log–log plots with the logarithm of the varying parameter (i.e.,
K
,
\gamma
, or
n
) on the
X
-axis and the logarithm of the MSE on the
Y
-axis. The slope of each segment in these plots can be interpreted as the convergence rate. Throughout the simulations, the shrinkage matrix
Q
is set to its oracle value, and we fix the number of covariates to be
p=2
.

Phase transition by varying
K
.
In this part, we vary
K
to highlight its effect on the phase transition in the convergence rate of
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})
. We assume
\gamma\gg n^{-1/(2\beta)}
in this setting. Under this regime, the MSE of
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})
satisfies

\mathrm{MSE}(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0}))\;\lesssim\;\begin{cases}n_{0}^{-1},&K\lesssim\gamma\,n_{0}^{1/(2\beta)},\\
\big(\tfrac{\gamma}{K}\big)^{2\beta},&\gamma\,n_{0}^{1/(2\beta)}\lesssim K\lesssim\gamma^{\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}}\,n^{1/(2\beta+1)},\\
\big(\tfrac{\gamma}{n}\big)^{\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}},&K\gtrsim\gamma^{\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}}\,n^{1/(2\beta+1)}.\end{cases}

Since
\gamma\gg n^{-1/(2\beta)}
implies
(\gamma/n)^{2\beta/(2\beta+1)}\gg n^{-1}
, the rate exhibits three distinct phases on a log–log scale with respect to
K
:
(i) a flat region at level
n_{0}^{-1}
for small
K
(as the rate does not depend on
K
),
(ii) a linear region with slope
-2\beta
, and
(iii) another linear region with slope
-2\beta/(2\beta+1)
for large
K
.
As a numerical validation, we conduct simulations under the same data-generating process across linear, logistic, and Poisson models, this time varying
K
while fixing
\bar{n}_{S}=1500
,
\gamma=0.1
, and
n_{0}=30
. For computing
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})
, the bandwidth is chosen as described in Equation (
3.1
). Figure
4
presents the resulting log–log plot of
\log\mathrm{MSE}
against
\log K
. As expected, we observe three distinct linear phases in the plot, along with their corresponding empirical slopes, which align closely with the theoretical predictions of
-2\beta=-4
and
-2\beta/(2\beta+1)=-0.8
.

Figure 4
:

Log–log plot of MSE of
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\rm TL}
as a function of
K
, while keeping
(\bar{n}_{S},\gamma)
fixed. Vertical dotted lines indicate empirical breakpoints that mark phase transitions in the convergence behavior. The left, middle, and right panels correspond to the linear, logistic, and Poisson models, respectively.

Phase transition by varying
\gamma
. We now illustrate the phase transition in the convergence rate of
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})
by varying
\gamma
. Based on Theorem
3.6
, the rate can be decomposed as follows:

\textstyle\mathrm{MSE}(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0}))\;\lesssim\;\begin{cases}n^{-1},&\gamma\lesssim n^{-1/(2\beta)},\\[4.0pt]
n^{-\tfrac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}}\gamma^{\tfrac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}},&n^{-1/(2\beta)}\lesssim\gamma\lesssim\min\!\Big\{K^{\frac{2\beta+1}{2\beta}}\,n^{-1/(2\beta)},\;n\,n_{0}^{-\frac{2\beta+1}{2\beta}}\Big\},\\[6.0pt]
n_{0}^{-1},&\min\!\Big\{K^{\frac{2\beta+1}{2\beta}}\,n^{-1/(2\beta)},\;n\,n_{0}^{-\frac{2\beta+1}{2\beta}}\Big\}\;\lesssim\;\gamma\lesssim K^{\frac{2\beta+1}{2\beta}}\,n^{-1/(2\beta)},\\[6.0pt]
K^{-2\beta}\gamma^{2\beta},&K^{\frac{2\beta+1}{2\beta}}\,n^{-1/(2\beta)}\lesssim\gamma\lesssim K\,n_{0}^{-1/(2\beta)},\\[6.0pt]
n_{0}^{-1},&\gamma\gtrsim K\,n_{0}^{-1/(2\beta)}.\end{cases}

(4.2)

In this simulation study, we assume
\bar{n}\gg n_{0}
, i.e., the target sample size is much smaller than the average source sample size. Under this regime, we have
K^{(2\beta+1)/2\beta}n^{-1/2\beta}\ll nn_{0}^{-(2\beta+1)/2\beta}
, which eliminates the third phase in Equation (
4.2
). Consequently, the log–log plot (with
\log\gamma
on the
X
-axis and
\log\mathrm{MSE}(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0}))
on the
Y
-axis) exhibits four distinct phases:(i) a flat region at level
n^{-1}
for small
\gamma
;
(ii) a linear growth with slope
2\beta/(2\beta+1)
;
(iii) a second linear growth with slope
2\beta
; and
(iv) a flat region at level
n_{0}^{-1}
for large
\gamma
.
Figure
5
illustrates this behavior. We set the average source sample size to
\bar{n}_{S}=600
, the target sample size to
n_{0}=30
, and use the oracle choice of
Q
. The number of domains is set to
K=12
for the linear model and
K=10
for the other settings. The functional coefficients are specified as
\theta_{0}(u)=\theta_{1}(u)=\tanh\big(8(u-0.2)\big)
. As predicted by the theory, the figure displays clear transitions across the four regimes, with the empirical slopes in the two linear phases closely matching the theoretical values
2\beta/(2\beta+1)=0.8
and
2\beta=4
(for
\beta=2
).

Figure 5
:

Log–log plot of MSE of
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\rm TL}
as a function of
\gamma
, while keeping
(n,K)
fixed. Vertical dotted lines indicate empirical breakpoints that mark phase transitions in the convergence behavior. The left, middle, and right panels correspond to the linear, logistic, and Poisson models, respectively.

Phase transition by varying
\bar{n}_{S}
. We now fix
(n_{0},K,\gamma)
and vary the average source sample size
\bar{n}_{S}
, so that the total source sample size is
n_{S}=K\bar{n}_{S}
and the overall sample size is
n=n_{0}+n_{S}
. Under the same condition
\gamma\gg n^{-1/(2\beta)}
, the MSE of
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})
exhibits the following phase transition in its convergence rate:

\textstyle\mathrm{MSE}(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0}))\;\lesssim\;\begin{cases}n_{0}^{-1},&n\,\lesssim\,\gamma n_{0}^{\frac{2\beta+1}{2\beta}},\\[8.0pt]
\left(\dfrac{\gamma}{n}\right)^{\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}},&\gamma n_{0}^{\frac{2\beta+1}{2\beta}}\,\lesssim\,n\,\lesssim\,\gamma^{-2\beta}K^{2\beta+1},\\[12.0pt]
n_{0}^{-1}\wedge\left(\dfrac{\gamma}{K}\right)^{2\beta},&n\,\gtrsim\,\gamma^{-2\beta}K^{2\beta+1}.\end{cases}

Consequently, on a log–log scale (with
\log n
on the
X
-axis and
\log\mathrm{MSE}(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0}))
on the
Y
-axis), the curve exhibits three distinct phases:
(i) a flat region at level
n_{0}^{-1}
for small
n
;
(ii) a linear regime with slope
-2\beta/(2\beta+1)
; and
(iii) a second plateau at level
n_{0}^{-1}\wedge(\gamma/K)^{2\beta}
for sufficiently large
n
.
Figure
6
illustrates this behavior with
\gamma=0.1
fixed while varying
\bar{n}_{S}
. We observe a short flat region followed by a linear regime whose empirical slope is close to
-2\beta/(2\beta+1)=-0.8
(for
\beta=2
). The third plateau emerges only when
n
becomes extremely large, which is consistent with the transition scale
\gamma^{-2\beta}K^{2\beta+1}
. Across the three models, we use
K=10
(linear),
K=3
(logistic), and
K=2
(Poisson), reflecting differing saturation behaviors in the third phase.

Figure 6
:

Log–log plot of MSE as a function of the sample size
n
. The vertical dotted lines represent the breakpoints for phase transition.
The left, middle, and right panels correspond to the linear, logistic, and Poisson models, respectively.

5

Real data analysis

In this section, we apply our methods to two real datasets to illustrate the performance of our proposed estimator. The first dataset is SLID-Ontario dataset (Subsection
5.1
); it is an economic dataset, where we want to predict a person’s composite hourly wage using their demographic attributes. The second dataset is US Adult Income dataset (Subsection
5.2
), where the goal is to predict whether a person’s yearly wage is greater than 50,000 based on their various attributes.
In both of these studies, we take
U
(domain identifier) to be the
years of employment
, as this typically determines an individual’s base salary.
For the simplicity of the implementation, we include two covariates in studies: i)
X_{1}
is gender, which is a binary variable (1 if female, 0 if male), and ii)
X_{2}
is years of education, as these two variables are known to affect the income quite significantly. Mathematically speaking, we fit the following (generalized) linear model:

g(\mathbb{E}(Y|\mathbf{X}))=\theta_{0}(U)+\theta_{1}(U)X_{1}+\theta_{2}(U)X_{2},

(5.1)

where
g
is a link function,
Y
is the response variable (composite hourly wage in SLID-Ontario dataset, and indicator whether annual income is
>50,000
in adult income dataset). Noticing that some values of
U
are unrealistic outliers such as negative
years of employment
, we remove all data points where
U
falls outside the “
3\sigma
” region. The retention rates are 99.97% (3996/3997) for SLID-Ontario and 99.53% (48615/48842) for US Adult Income.

5.1

Application 1: Survey of Labour and Income Dynamics in Ontario

The first dataset we analyze is a public-use sample from the 1994 Survey of Labour and Income Dynamics in Ontario (SLID-Ontario)
[
19
]
. The dataset contains information on four attributes for 3997 individuals:
age
,
gender
,
years of education
, and
composite hourly wage
. In this application, we study the predictive relationship between the logarithm of the
composite hourly wage
(the response variable
Y
) and other covariates. Following
[
19
]
, we use the log-transformed wage to mitigate non-normality. We employ the following domain-varying-coefficient model

\textstyle\mathbb{E}[Y\mid X,U]=\theta_{0}(U)+\theta_{1}(U)X_{1}+\theta_{2}(U)X_{2},

(5.2)

where
X_{1}
is a gender indicator and
X_{2}
denotes years of education. We approximate the
years of employment

U
by
U=\textit{age}-\textit{years of education}-6
, assuming individuals begin schooling at age six and enter the workforce immediately after graduation. To ensure scale invariance and comparability across domains, we normalize
U
via the min–max transformation

\textstyle U\leftarrow\frac{U-\min_{1\leq i\leq n}U_{i}}{\max_{1\leq i\leq n}U_{i}-\min_{1\leq i\leq n}U_{i}},

(5.3)

so that the domain identifiers lie in
[0,1]
.

To construct source and target domains, we discretize
U
into ten bins
[0,0.1],(0.1,0.2],\ldots,(0.9,1]
, and map each
U_{i}
to the midpoint of its bin. Let
\mathcal{U}^{*}=\{0.05,0.15,\ldots,0.95\}
denote the set of bin midpoints. Each midpoint defines a domain identifier. Specifically, domain
j
consists of all observations satisfying
U_{i}\in((j-1)/10,\,j/10]
and its associated identifier is
U_{j}^{*}=(j-0.5)/10
. For each
u_{0}\in\mathcal{U}^{*}
, we designate the corresponding domain as the target domain
\mathcal{D}_{0}
and treat the remaining observations as the source domain
\mathcal{D}_{S}
. Our objective is to evaluate predictive performance on the target domain and assess whether borrowing information from nearby domains improves accuracy without inducing negative transfer. Towards that goal, we randomly split the target domain (of size
m
) into three equal parts
\{\mathcal{D}_{0}^{j}\}_{j=1}^{3}
, where
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{1}\cup\mathcal{D}_{0}^{2}
is used for training and
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{3}
is reserved for testing. In our experiment, we compare performance of three estimators:

1.

Target-only baseline

\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{LR}}(u_{0})
, fitted using
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{1}\cup\mathcal{D}_{0}^{2}
.

2.

Nonparametric DVCM

\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})
, computed using pooled data
\mathcal{D}_{S}\cup\mathcal{D}_{0}^{1}\cup\mathcal{D}_{0}^{2}
via local polynomial regression.

3.

Adaptive transfer-learning estimator

\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})
.

To construct
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})
, we first compute a pilot estimator
\tilde{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})
using
\mathcal{D}_{S}\cup\mathcal{D}_{0}^{1}
. We then fine-tune this estimate using
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{2}
by solving

\textstyle\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})=\argmin_{{\bm{\alpha}}}\frac{1}{m/3}\sum_{(X_{ki},Y_{ki})\in\mathcal{D}_{0}^{2}}\left(Y_{ki}-X_{ki}^{\top}{\bm{\alpha}}\right)^{2}+\|{\bm{\alpha}}-\tilde{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\|_{\hat{Q}}^{2}.

The data-splitting ensures independence between the pilot estimator and the refinement step, as discussed in Section
3
. The penalty matrix
\hat{Q}
is estimated as in (
2.14
). We then evaluate the predictive performance on the test set
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{3}
using

\textstyle\mathrm{MSE}(u_{0},\hat{\bm{\theta}})=\frac{1}{m/3}\sum_{(X_{i},Y_{i})\in\mathcal{D}_{0}^{3}}(X_{i}^{\top}\hat{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})-Y_{i})^{2}.

To reduce variability due to random splitting, we repeat the procedure ten times and report the average MSE.

Each value in
\mathcal{U}^{*}
is treated in turn as the target domain, yielding a trajectory
\mathrm{MSE}(u_{0},\hat{\bm{\theta}})
across
u_{0}
. The three estimators
\{\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{LR}},\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}},\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}\}
produce the trajectories shown in Figure
7
(left panel), while the right panel displays the distribution of the unbinned
U_{i}
values. Exact numerical MSE can be found in Appendix
E
.

Several patterns emerge from Figure
7
. First, the target-only estimator
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{LR}}(u_{0})
exhibits large MSE near the right boundary (
u_{0}\approx 1
), reflecting data scarcity in that region, as seen in the histogram. Second,
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})
performs worse near the left boundary, where the target domain itself contains abundant data and therefore the target-only baseline itself is a strong predictor. Last but not least, the adaptive estimator
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})
automatically tracks the better of the two estimators; when
u_{0}
is small, it behaves similarly to
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{LR}}(u_{0})
; when
u_{0}
is large, it aligns more closely with
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})
. Across all target domains,
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})
achieves the most stable and favorable performance, corroborating both our theoretical results and simulation findings.

5.2

Application 2: US Adult Income

The US Adult Income dataset (also known as the “Census Income” or “Adult” dataset) contains demographic attributes and income levels for
48,842
individuals from the 1994 US Census. It is widely used in the machine learning literature, particularly in studies of classification performance and algorithmic fairness (e.g., see
[
48
,
30
,
49
]
), where the goal is to predict whether an individual earns more than $50,000 per year. To maintain consistency with the previous subsection, we select three covariates, age, gender, and years of education—to predict the binary response variable
Y
. We model the response using the generalized linear DVCM

\textstyle{\mathbb{P}}(Y=1\mid X_{1},X_{2},U)=\frac{1}{1+{\sf exp}\left(-\{\theta_{0}(U)+\theta_{1}(U)X_{1}+\theta_{2}(U)X_{2}\}\right)}.

(5.4)

Here,
Y=1
indicates annual income
\geq 50{,}000
, and
Y=0
otherwise. As in Section
5.1
, we approximate years of employment
U
and construct domain identifiers using the same binning and scaling procedure, resulting in
10
domains indexed by
\mathcal{U}^{*}=\{0.05,0.15,\ldots,0.95\}
. For a given
u_{0}\in\mathcal{U}^{*}
, the observations with
U_{k}^{*}=u_{0}
form the target domain
\mathcal{D}_{0}
(of size
m
), while the remaining observations constitute the source domain
\mathcal{D}_{S}
. The target domain is randomly split into training subsets
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{1}
and
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{2}
and a test subset
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{3}
, each of size
m/3
. Here also, we compare three estimators: i)
the target-only baseline

\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{GLR}}(u_{0})
(constructed using
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{1}\cup\mathcal{D}_{0}^{2}
), ii)
the non-parametric generalized DVCM

\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})
(constructed using
\mathcal{D}_{S}\cup\mathcal{D}_{0}^{1}\cup\mathcal{D}_{0}^{2}
), and iii)
our proposed transfer-learning estimator

\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})
, which, as in the previous subsection, constructed in two steps: first, we compute a pilot non-parametric DVCM estimator
\tilde{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})
using
\mathcal{D}_{S}\cup\mathcal{D}_{0}^{1}
. We then refine this estimate on
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{2}
by solving

\textstyle\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})=\argmin_{{\bm{\alpha}}}\frac{1}{m/3}\sum_{(X_{ki},Y_{ki})\in\mathcal{D}_{0}^{2}}\ell(X_{ki}^{\top}{\bm{\alpha}},Y_{ki})+\frac{1}{2}\|{\bm{\alpha}}-\tilde{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\|_{\hat{Q}}^{2},

where
\ell(\cdot,\cdot)
denotes the cross-entropy loss and the penalty matrix
\hat{Q}
is chosen as in Equation (
2.14
). To reduce variability due to random splitting, we repeat the procedure ten times and report the average cross-entropy loss on the test set
\mathcal{D}_{0}^{3}
. Each value in
\mathcal{U}^{*}
is treated in turn as the target domain, yielding trajectories of predictive error across
u_{0}
. The three estimators
\{\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{GLR}},\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}},\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}\}
produce the curves shown in Figure
8
(left panel), while the right panel displays the distribution of the unbinned
U_{i}
values. Exact numerical results are provided in Appendix
E
.

The qualitative behavior mirrors that observed in Section
5.1
. The logistic regression estimator
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{GLR}}(u_{0})
performs particularly well near the left endpoint (
u_{0}\approx 0
), whereas in other regions the performance of the nonparametric estimator
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})
is at par. The adaptive estimator
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})
consistently aligns with the better-performing method across domains, highlighting its ability to automatically balance between pooling and target-only learning.

Figure 7
:
Model evaluation on SLID-Ontario dataset. The MSEs of the linear regression, varying-coefficient model, and transfer learning estimator at different values of
u_{0}
based on bandwidth of 0.2 are shown on the left. The histogram of the scaled year of employment,
U
, is shown on the right.

Figure 8
:
Model evaluation on the US Adult Income dataset. The cross-entropy of the logistic regression, logistic-based varying-coefficient, and transfer-learning estimators at different values of
u_{0}
based on bandwidth of 0.2 is shown on the left. The histogram of the scaled year of employment,
U
, is shown on the right.

6

Conclusion and future work

We study multi-source transfer learning under
posterior drift
, where the conditional relationship between response and covariates varies across environments indexed by a domain identifier
U
. To capture this structured heterogeneity, we introduce a
domain–varying coefficient model
(DVCM) and propose a two-step estimator that combines nonparametric pooling across source domains with a ridge-type fine-tuning step on the target domain. Our main contribution is a data-adaptive choice of the shrinkage matrix
Q
that provably prevents negative transfer: the resulting estimator automatically interpolates between target-only and pooled estimators and never incurs higher risk than the target-only baseline. We establish matching minimax upper and lower bounds for estimating
\theta(u_{0})
, revealing a phase transition governed by smoothness, domain dispersion, and the number of source environments. We further derive asymptotic normality with feasible variance estimation, enabling valid confidence intervals and hypothesis tests. Simulations and real-data experiments confirm that the procedure adaptively tracks the better of target-only and pooled learning across regimes. However, several interesting directions remain open for future investigation:

1.

Beyond linear models in
X
. In this paper, we focused on models that are linear in
X
. A natural extension is to consider more flexible non-linear structures, such as single-index models
\mathbb{E}[Y\mid X,U]=g(X^{\top}\theta(U))
for unknown link
g
, or additive models of the form
\mathbb{E}[Y\mid X,U]=\sum_{j=1}^{d}\theta_{j}(U)f_{j}(X)
, where both
\theta_{j}(\cdot)
and
f_{j}(\cdot)
are unknown. An important theoretical question is whether the negative-transfer robustness and adaptive minimax optimality established here continue to hold under suitable reformulations of the estimation procedure for such broader nonparametric classes.

2.

Modern machine learning estimators for the
U
-varying component. We estimated the nonparametric components via local polynomial regression. A promising direction is to investigate neural network or transformer-based estimators for learning
\theta(U)
, especially when
U
is multi-dimensional. As observed in recent work (e.g., see
[
37
,
25
]
), neural network estimators can adapt to compositional structures and mitigate the curse of dimensionality. Understanding whether similar adaptivity and phase-transition phenomena persist under modern deep-learning architectures remains an important open problem.

3.

High-dimensional covariates and structured sparsity.
Our analysis assumes that the dimension of
X
is fixed. In growing/high-dimensional settings, variable selection becomes essential, particularly when only a subset of covariates is informative, and the sparsity
pattern may vary across domains. One natural extension is to incorporate sparsity-inducing penalties
(e.g.,
\ell_{1}
-regularization or structured group penalties) into the domain-adaptive framework. Alternatively, domain heterogeneity may be captured through a low-rank latent factor structure. Developing adaptive procedures that combine transfer learning with sparsity or low-rank structure on the covariates would substantially broaden the scope of the model.

4.

Bayesian formulations and adaptive borrowing. A Bayesian perspective offers another appealing direction. One may model the domain identifiers
U_{i}
as draws from a prior distribution (where the prior variance encodes cross-domain similarity), and the coefficient function
\theta(\cdot)
is generated from a nonparametric prior (e.g., Gaussian process, spline-based prior, or Bayesian neural network prior) that models the smoothness. An important theoretical question is how to modify the likelihood equation appropriately so that the resulting posterior achieves adaptive contraction rates that match the minimax frequentist rates derived here. If so, one may use this approach for uncertainty quantification from a Bayesian perspective.

Appendix

Throughout the theoretical analysis in the Appendix, we assume a target sample-splitting procedure. Specifically, we observe a total of
2n_{0}
target samples, which are partitioned into two equal subsets satisfying
|\mathcal{I}_{0}|=|\mathcal{I}_{0}^{*}|=n_{0}
. The subset
\mathcal{I}_{0}
is used to construct the initial nonparametric estimator, while
\mathcal{I}_{0}^{*}
is reserved for the subsequent fine-tuning step. Under this setup, we first analyze the estimator in the linear response setting. The nonparametric estimator
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})
is defined as

\textstyle\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})=\mathbf{A}_{l}\cdot\argmin_{{\bm{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{R}^{(l+1)p}}\sum_{k=0}^{K}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}\left[Y_{ki}-\left({\bm{\Phi}}_{l}\!\left(\frac{U_{k}-u_{0}}{h}\right)^{\top}\otimes X_{ki}^{\top}\right){\bm{\alpha}}\right]^{2}W\!\left(\frac{U_{k}-u_{0}}{h}\right),

(.1)

where
{\bm{\Phi}}_{l}(x)=\big(1,x,x^{2}/2!,\ldots,x^{l}/l!\big)^{\top}
denotes the
l
-th order polynomial feature map,
\otimes
is the Kronecker product, and
\mathbf{A}_{l}=[I_{p},\mathbf{0}_{p\times lp}]
extracts the first
p
coordinates of the minimizer.

The fine-tuning estimator constructed from
\mathcal{I}_{0}^{*}
is then given by

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})

\displaystyle=\argmin_{{\bm{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}}\frac{1}{2n_{0}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{0}^{*}}\big(Y_{0i}-X_{0i}^{\top}{\bm{\alpha}}\big)^{2}+\frac{1}{2}\|{\bm{\alpha}}-\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\|_{Q}^{2}

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{n_{0}}\mathbf{X}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{0}+Q\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{n_{0}}\mathbf{X}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{0}+Q\,\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right).

(.2)

Similarly, for the generalized linear response model, the Step I estimator is defined as

\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{GDVCM}}(u_{0})=\mathbf{A}_{l}\cdot\argmin_{{\bm{\alpha}}\in\mathbb{R}^{(l+1)p}}\sum_{k=0}^{K}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}\ell\!\big(Z_{ki}^{\top}\alpha,Y_{ki}\big)\,W\!\left(\frac{U_{k}-u_{0}}{h}\right),

(.3)

while the Step II fine-tuning estimator based on
\mathcal{I}_{0}^{*}
is

\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})=\argmin_{{\bm{\alpha}}}\frac{1}{n_{0}}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{0}^{*}}\ell\!\big(X_{0i}^{\top}\alpha,Y_{0i}\big)+\frac{1}{2}\|{\bm{\alpha}}-\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{GDVCM}}(u_{0})\|_{Q}^{2}\,.

(.4)

This appendix, centered on the estimators defined above, is organized as follows.
Section
A
collects auxiliary lemmas used throughout the paper. Section
B
contains proofs of the main theorems, propositions, and lemmas from the main body. Section
C
provides the proofs of the auxiliary lemmas.

Appendix A

Auxiliary lemmas

In this section we collect auxiliary lemmas that support the main results. Section
A.1
gathers tools for the nonasymptotic analysis: Lemmas
A.1
–
A.4
serve as building blocks for Proposition
3.5
. Section
A.2
contains asymptotic tools: Lemma
A.5
underpins Theorem
3.13
and Proposition
A.9
; Lemma
A.6
is utilized to prove Proposition
A.8
; Lemma
A.10
is used in the proof of Theorem
3.13
; and Lemma
A.11
aids Theorem
3.14
.

A.1

Auxiliary lemmas for nonasymptotic analysis

Recall the linear DVCM estimator is

\textstyle\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})=\mathbf{A}_{l}(\mathbf{Z}^{\top}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{Z})^{-1}\mathbf{Z}^{\top}\mathbf{W}\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{R}^{p}\,.

(A.1)

Let us first recall some basic notations:

\displaystyle\mathbf{Z}^{\top}=\left[Z_{01},Z_{02},\ldots,Z_{0n_{0}},\ldots,Z_{K1},Z_{K2},\ldots,Z_{Kn_{K}}\right],

\displaystyle\mathbf{W}=S_{h}^{-1}{\sf diag}\bigg\{\underbrace{W\left(\frac{U_{0}-u_{0}}{h}\right),\ldots,W\left(\frac{U_{0}-u_{0}}{h}\right)}_{n_{0}\text{ identical terms}},\ldots,\underbrace{W\left(\frac{U_{K}-u_{0}}{h}\right),\ldots,W\left(\frac{U_{K}-u_{0}}{h}\right)}_{n_{K}\text{ identical terms}}\bigg\},

\displaystyle\mathbf{y}^{\top}=\left[\mathbf{y}_{0}^{\top},\ldots,\mathbf{y}_{K}^{\top}\right],

where the normalizing constant
S_{h}=\sum_{k=0}^{K}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}W\left(\frac{U_{k}-u_{0}}{h}\right)
, and
W
is a uniform kernel (Assumption
3.4
).
The following lemma provides a finite sample concentration inequality on the distance of
u_{0}
from its nearest and furthest neighbors, i.e.,
d_{(1)}(u_{0})
and
d_{(K)}(u_{0})
. In particular, it shows that
d_{(1)}(u_{0})
and
d_{(K)}(u_{0})
are of the order
\gamma/K
and
\gamma
.

Lemma A.1

Under Assumption
3.2
(a), the following bounds hold:

(1)

\left[1-\frac{2a_{0}t}{K}\right]^{K}\mathbf{1}\left\{t\leq K/(2a_{0})\right\}\leq{\mathbb{P}}\left(Kd_{(1)}(u_{0})>\gamma t\right)\leq\left[1-\frac{2a_{0}^{\prime}t}{K}\right]^{K}\mathbf{1}\left\{t\leq K/(2a_{0}^{\prime})\right\},

C_{1}\left(K/\gamma\right)^{-2\beta}\leq\mathbb{E}\left[d_{(1)}^{2\beta}(u_{0})\right]\leq C_{2}\left(K/\gamma\right)^{-2\beta}.

(2)

\bigg\{1-\left[\frac{2a_{0}t}{K}\right]^{K}\bigg\}\mathbf{1}\left\{t\leq K/(2a_{0})\right\}\leq{\mathbb{P}}\left(Kd_{(K)}(u_{0})>\gamma t\right)\leq\bigg\{1-\left[\frac{2a_{0}^{\prime}t}{K}\right]^{K}\bigg\}\mathbf{1}\left\{t\leq K/(2a_{0}^{\prime})\right\},

C_{3}\gamma^{2\beta}\leq\mathbb{E}\left[d_{(K)}^{2\beta}(u_{0})\right]\leq C_{4}\gamma^{2\beta}.

The proof on part (1) is in Appendix
C.1
and the proof on part (2) is in Appendix
C.2
. The following Lemma shows that under our assumptions, the random variables
Z_{ki}
are upper bounded almost surely under certain constraints.

Lemma A.2

Under Assumptions
3.2
and
3.4
, it holds that

\mathbf{1}\{|U_{k}-u_{0}|\leq h\}\left\|Z_{ki}\right\|_{2}\leq 2\,.

The proof is in Appendix
C.3
.

Lemma A.3

Let Assumptions
3.2
–
3.4
hold and
h
be such that
h\leq|\mathcal{U}|
. Then there exists a constant
C>0
such that

\mathbb{E}\left[S_{h}^{-1}\mid d_{(1)}(u_{0})\leq h\right]\leq C\frac{\gamma}{nh}\,.

The proof is in Appendix
C.4
. Recall that
\Gamma=\{U_{k}:0\leq k\leq K\}\cup\{X_{ki}:0\leq k\leq K,i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}\}
is the set of all covariates. The following lemma shows that the
\mathrm{MSE}_{A}
(conditioning on
\Gamma
) of the DVCM estimator is of order
O(h^{2\beta}+S_{h}^{-1})
.

Lemma A.4

Under Assumptions
3.1
–
3.4
, the following upper bound holds:

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}\left[\|\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\|_{A}^{2}\middle|\Gamma\right]\leq q_{1}^{2}h^{2\beta}+q_{2}S_{h}^{-1},\end{split}

(A.2)

where
q_{1},q_{2}>0
are constants, and
S_{h}=(1/2)\sum_{k=0}^{K}n_{k}\mathbf{1}\left\{|U_{k}-u_{0}|\leq h\right\}
.

The proof of this lemma is in Appendix
C.5
.

A.2

Auxiliary lemmas for asymptotic analysis

The next lemma provides asymptotic expressions for the conditional mean and variance of the quantities
\Delta_{k}
and
\Lambda_{k}
in the GDVCM setting. These expansions serve as essential building blocks for the subsequent asymptotic analysis.

Lemma A.5

Under Assumptions
3.1

2′′
,
3.3
, and
3.4
, define for
t_{k}=(u_{k}-u_{0})/h
,

\bar{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})^{\top}=\big[{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})^{\top},\,h{\bm{\theta}}^{\prime}(u_{0})^{\top},\,\ldots,\,h^{l}{\bm{\theta}}^{(l)}(u_{0})^{\top}\big],

\displaystyle\Delta_{k}

\displaystyle=n_{k}^{-1/2}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}s_{1}\big(Z_{ki}^{\top}\bar{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0}),Y_{ki}\big)\,Z_{ki}\,W(t_{k}),

\displaystyle\Lambda_{k}

\displaystyle=n_{k}^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}s_{2}\big(Z_{ki}^{\top}\bar{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0}),Y_{ki}\big)\,Z_{ki}Z_{ki}^{\top}\,W(t_{k}),

where
Z_{ki}={\bm{\Phi}}_{l}(t_{k})\otimes X_{ki}
and
s_{j}(\eta,y)=\partial^{j}\ell(\eta,y)/\partial\eta^{j}
. Then, for any
k\in\{0\}\cup[K]
and sufficiently small
h>0
, and some
\tilde{u}_{k}
satisfying
|\tilde{u}_{k}-u_{0}|\leq h

\displaystyle n_{k}^{-1/2}\,\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{k}\mid U_{k}=u_{k}]

\displaystyle={\bm{\Phi}}_{l}(t_{k})^{\otimes 2}\otimes\Psi(u_{k})\,\mathbf{A}_{l}^{\top}\frac{{\bm{\theta}}^{(l)}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}^{(l)}(\tilde{u}_{k})}{l!}\,(t_{k}h)^{l}\,W(t_{k})\{1+o(1)\}\lesssim h^{\beta}

\displaystyle{\sf Var}[\Delta_{k}\mid U_{k}=u_{k}]

\displaystyle=\nu(u_{k})\,{\bm{\Phi}}_{l}(t_{k})^{\otimes 2}\otimes\Psi(u_{k})\,W(t_{k})^{2}+O(h^{\beta}),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\Lambda_{k}\mid U_{k}=u_{k}]

\displaystyle={\bm{\Phi}}_{l}(t_{k})^{\otimes 2}\otimes\Psi(u_{k})\,W(t_{k})+O(h^{\beta}).

The proof of this lemma is found in Appendix
C.6
. The next lemma (on linear DVCM) is an immediate specialization of Lemma
A.5
(on generalized DVCM) obtained by choosing the quadratic loss
\ell(\eta,y)=\tfrac{1}{2}(\eta-y)^{2}
. In this case
s_{2}\equiv 1
and
\Psi(u)=\mathbb{E}[XX^{\top}\mid U=u]
; moreover the GLR scale function
\nu(u)
is replaced by the noise variance
\sigma^{2}(u)
. Hence all conclusions of Lemma
A.5
remain the same, with the only substitution
\sigma^{2}(\cdot)=\nu(\cdot)
.

Lemma A.6

Under Assumptions
3.1
,
2′
,
3.3
, and
3.4
, define for
t_{k}=(u_{k}-u_{0})/h
,

\bar{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})^{\top}=\big[{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})^{\top},\,h{\bm{\theta}}^{\prime}(u_{0})^{\top},\,\ldots,\,h^{l}{\bm{\theta}}^{(l)}(u_{0})^{\top}\big],

\displaystyle\Delta_{k}

\displaystyle=n_{k}^{-1/2}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}s_{1}\big(Z_{ki}^{\top}\bar{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0}),Y_{ki}\big)\,Z_{ki}\,W(t_{k}),

\displaystyle\Lambda_{k}

\displaystyle=n_{k}^{-1}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}s_{2}\big(Z_{ki}^{\top}\bar{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0}),Y_{ki}\big)\,Z_{ki}Z_{ki}^{\top}\,W(t_{k}),

where
Z_{ki}={\bm{\Phi}}_{l}(t_{k})\otimes X_{ki}
and
s_{j}(\eta,y)=\partial^{j}\ell(\eta,y)/\partial\eta^{j}
. Then, for any
k\in\{0\}\cup[K]
and sufficiently small
h>0
, and some
\tilde{u}_{k}
satisfying
|\tilde{u}_{k}-u_{0}|\leq h

\displaystyle n_{k}^{-1/2}\,\mathbb{E}[\Delta_{k}\mid U_{k}=u_{k}]

\displaystyle={\bm{\Phi}}_{l}(t_{k})^{\otimes 2}\otimes\Psi(u_{k})\,\mathbf{A}_{l}^{\top}\frac{{\bm{\theta}}^{(l)}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}^{(l)}(\tilde{u}_{k})}{l!}\,(t_{k}h)^{l}\,W(t_{k})\{1+o(1)\}\lesssim h^{\beta}

\displaystyle{\sf Var}[\Delta_{k}\mid U_{k}=u_{k}]

\displaystyle=\sigma^{2}(u_{k})\,{\bm{\Phi}}_{l}(t_{k})^{\otimes 2}\otimes\Psi(u_{k})\,W(t_{k})^{2}+O(h^{\beta}),

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\Lambda_{k}\mid U_{k}=u_{k}]

\displaystyle={\bm{\Phi}}_{l}(t_{k})^{\otimes 2}\otimes\Psi(u_{k})\,W(t_{k})+O(h^{\beta}).

Proof A.7

This corollary on linear DVCM is a special case of Lemma
A.5
(on GDVCM). Therefore, Lemma
A.5
may be directly applied, with the only difference being variance function
\nu(\cdot)
replaced by
\sigma^{2}(\cdot)
.

The next proposition establishes the asymptotic distribution of the two base estimators
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{LR}}(u_{0})
and
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})
.

Proposition A.8

Under Assumptions
3.1
,
2′
,
3.3
, and
3.4
, the target-only estimator
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{LR}}(u_{0})
satisfies:

\sqrt{n_{0}}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{LR}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\right)\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}\left(0,\Omega_{\mathrm{LR}}(u_{0})\right),\quad\Omega_{\mathrm{LR}}(u_{0})=\sigma^{2}(u_{0})\,\Psi(u_{0})^{-1}\,.

Furthermore, if
h
is such that
d_{(1)}(u_{0})\leq h\leq d_{(K)}(u_{0})
and

\frac{\gamma}{K}\ll h\lesssim\Big(\frac{\gamma}{n}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}
, then
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})
satisfies:

\sqrt{\frac{nh}{\gamma}}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})-\mathbf{b}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}\left(0,\Omega_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)\,,

where

\displaystyle\mathbf{b}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})

\displaystyle\lesssim h^{\beta},

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})

\displaystyle=\sigma^{2}(u_{0})\left[\zeta_{0,1}^{-1}\,\zeta_{0,2}\,\zeta_{0,1}^{-1}\right]_{1,1}\Psi(u_{0})^{-1},

\displaystyle\zeta_{r,s}

\displaystyle=\int{\bm{\Phi}}_{l}(t)^{\otimes 2}t^{r}W^{s}(t)f\Big(\tfrac{u_{0}-u^{*}+ht}{\gamma}\Big)\,dt\,,

u^{*}
is same as defined in Assumption
3.2
.

Proposition A.9

Under Assumptions
3.1
,
2′′
,
3.3
, and
3.4
, the target-only estimator
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{GLR}}(u_{0})
satisfies:

\sqrt{n_{0}}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{GLR}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\right)\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}\left(0,\Omega_{\mathrm{GLR}}(u_{0})\right),\quad\Omega_{\mathrm{GLR}}(u_{0})=\nu(u_{0})\,\Psi(u_{0})^{-1}\,.

Furthermore, if
h
is such that
d_{(1)}(u_{0})\leq h\leq d_{(K)}(u_{0})
and

\frac{\gamma}{K}\ll h\lesssim\Big(\frac{\gamma}{n}\Big)^{\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}
,
then
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{GDVCM}}(u_{0})
satisfies:

\sqrt{\frac{nh}{\gamma}}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{GDVCM}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})-\mathbf{b}_{\mathrm{GDVCM}}(u_{0})\right)\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}\left(0,\Omega_{\mathrm{GDVCM}}(u_{0})\right)\,,

where

\displaystyle\mathbf{b}_{\mathrm{GDVCM}}(u_{0})

\displaystyle\lesssim h^{\beta},

\displaystyle\Omega_{\mathrm{GDVCM}}(u_{0})

\displaystyle=\nu(u_{0})\left[\zeta_{0,1}^{-1}\,\zeta_{0,2}\,\zeta_{0,1}^{-1}\right]_{1,1}\Psi(u_{0})^{-1},

\displaystyle\zeta_{r,s}

\displaystyle=\int{\bm{\Phi}}_{l}(t)^{\otimes 2}t^{r}W^{s}(t)f\Big(\tfrac{u_{0}-u^{*}+ht}{\gamma}\Big)\,dt\,,

u^{*}
is same as defined in Assumption
3.2
.

Note that this result extends Proposition
A.8
, with

\sigma^{2}(\cdot)
replaced by
\nu(\cdot)
and with
\Psi(\cdot)
redefined accordingly. The proof is given in Appendix
C.7
.

The next lemma establishes, within the GDVCM framework, that if the shrinkage matrix
Q
is chosen to be of the same order as
\rho^{2}
(i.e.,
Q\asymp_{p}\rho^{2}I
) where
\rho:=r_{\mathrm{GLR}}/r_{\mathrm{GDVCM}}
is the ratio of the convergence rates of the target-only GLR estimator and the GDVCM estimator, then the TL estimator
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})
adapts to the faster procedure: specifically,

\|\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\|=O_{p}\big(r_{\mathrm{TL}}\big),\qquad r_{\mathrm{TL}}:=r_{\mathrm{GLR}}\wedge r_{\mathrm{GDVCM}}.

Moreover, if the bandwidth
h
satisfies the standard localization conditions, then
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})
attains the same asymptotic distribution as the faster estimator in Proposition
A.9
: if
\rho\to 0
,

\sqrt{n_{0}}\big(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\big)\to_{d}\mathcal{N}\big(0,\Omega_{\mathrm{GLR}}(u_{0})\big)
;
if
\rho\to\infty
,

\sqrt{nh/\gamma}\big(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})-\mathbf{b}_{\mathrm{GDVCM}}(u_{0})\big)\to_{d}\mathcal{N}\big(0,\Omega_{\mathrm{GDVCM}}(u_{0})\big)
.

Lemma A.10

Let Assumptions
3.1

2′′
,
3.3
, and
3.4
hold. Let the rates
r_{\mathrm{GLR}}
and
r_{\mathrm{GDVCM}}
be such that

\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{GLR}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})=O_{p}(r_{\mathrm{GLR}}),\quad\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{GDVCM}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})=O_{p}(r_{\mathrm{GDVCM}}).

Moreover, let
Q
be a positive-definite matrix satisfying for
\rho=r_{\mathrm{GLR}}/r_{\mathrm{GDVCM}}
,

c\rho^{2}\leq\lambda_{\min}(Q)\leq\lambda_{\max}(Q)\leq C\rho^{2}\quad\text{for constants }C>c>0,\quad\text{ w.p.}\to 1.

Then

\big\|\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\big\|_{2}=O_{p}(r_{\mathrm{GLR}}\wedge r_{\mathrm{GDVCM}})\,.

Furthermore, suppose
h
satisfies the same conditions in Proposition
A.9
, then:

\displaystyle\text{If }\rho\to 0,\quad\sqrt{n_{0}}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\right)\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}\left(0,\Omega_{\mathrm{GLR}}(u_{0})\right),

\displaystyle\text{If }\rho\to\infty,\quad\sqrt{\frac{nh}{\gamma}}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})-\mathbf{b}_{\mathrm{GDVCM}}(u_{0})\right)\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}\left(0,\Omega_{\mathrm{GDVCM}}(u_{0})\right),

where
\Omega_{\mathrm{GLR}}(u_{0})
,
\mathbf{b}_{\mathrm{GDVCM}}(u_{0})
, and
\Omega_{\mathrm{GDVCM}}(u_{0})
are defined in Proposition
A.9
.

The proof is given in Appendix
C.9
. The next lemma, which is under the GDVCM framework, states that if we choose
h
to be small enough, then the TL estimator achieves asymptotical normality with mean 0.

Lemma A.11

Under the same conditions of Lemma
A.10
, suppose that the bandwidth additionally satisfies

\frac{nh^{2\beta+1}}{\gamma}\to 0\,.

Then

\displaystyle\text{If }\rho\to 0,\quad\sqrt{n_{0}}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\right)\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}\left(0,\Omega_{\mathrm{GLR}}(u_{0})\right),

\displaystyle\text{If }\rho\to\infty,\quad\sqrt{\frac{nh}{\gamma}}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\right)\xrightarrow{d}\mathcal{N}\left(0,\Omega_{\mathrm{GDVCM}}(u_{0})\right),

where
\Omega_{\mathrm{GLR}}(u_{0})
,
\mathbf{b}_{\mathrm{GDVCM}}(u_{0})
, and
\Omega_{\mathrm{GDVCM}}(u_{0})
are defined in Proposition
A.9
.

Proof A.12

This lemma follows directly from Lemma
A.10
by choosing
h
such that
\sqrt{nh/\gamma}\,\mathbf{b}_{\mathrm{GDVCM}}\to 0
. Note that by Proposition
A.9
the bias
\mathbf{b}_{\mathrm{GDVCM}}
is of order
O(h^{\beta})
, so this is equivalent to requiring
nh^{2\beta+1}/\gamma\to 0
.

Appendix B

Proofs of main theorems

B.1

Proof of Proposition
3.5

Proof B.1

Recall that, we choose
h
according to Equation (
3.1
):

h={\sf med}\left\{e_{0}(n/\gamma)^{-\frac{1}{2\beta+1}},d_{(1)}(u_{0}),d_{(K)}(u_{0})\right\}\,.

Depending on the distribution of
\{u_{0},\dots,u_{K}\}
, any one of the three elements can be chosen as
h
. Based on this, we divide our analysis into three disjoint events:

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{1}

\displaystyle=\left\{e_{0}(n/\gamma)^{-\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}<d_{(1)}(u_{0})\right\}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{2}

\displaystyle=\left\{e_{0}(n/\gamma)^{-\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}>d_{(K)}(u_{0})\right\}\,,

\displaystyle\mathcal{E}_{3}

\displaystyle=\left\{d_{(1)}(u_{0})\leq e_{0}(n/\gamma)^{-\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}\leq d_{(K)}(u_{0})\right\}\,.

As
d_{(1)}(u_{0})\leq d_{(K)}(u_{0})
, it is immediate that we choose
h=d_{(1)}(u_{0})
under
\mathcal{E}_{1}
,
h=d_{(K)}(u_{0})
under
\mathcal{E}_{2}
and
h=e_{0}(n/\gamma)^{-1/(2\beta+1)}
under
\mathcal{E}_{3}
. Now, from Lemma
A.4
, we know that for any choice of
h
we have:

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}\left[\|\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\|_{A}^{2}\middle|\Gamma\right]\leq q_{1}^{2}h^{2\beta}+q_{2}S_{h}^{-1}.\end{split}

(B.1)

Therefore, by a simple law of total expectation, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\|\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\|_{A}^{2}\right]\leq\sum_{j=1}^{3}\mathbb{E}\left[q_{1}^{2}{h}^{2\beta}+q_{2}S_{h}^{-1}\mid\mathcal{E}_{j}\right]{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{E}_{j})\coloneqq\sum_{j=1}^{3}r_{j}p_{j}\,,

where, for notational simplicity, define
r_{j}=\mathbb{E}[q_{1}^{2}{h}^{2\beta}+q_{2}S_{h}^{-1}\mid\mathcal{E}_{j}]
and
p_{j}={\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{E}_{j})
. We next provide a bound on each
r_{j}
and
p_{j}
on a case-by-case basis.

First, consider
\mathcal{E}_{1}
: We choose
h=d_{(1)}(u_{0})
, so only the nearest source (in terms of
u
) is selected. As a consequence, by Assumption
3.3
a deterministic bound holds

S_{h}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{K}n_{k}\mathbf{1}\left\{|U_{k}-u_{0}|\leq h\right\}=\frac{1}{2}(n_{0}+n_{(1)})\lesssim\bar{n}\,.

where
n_{(1)}
is the number of samples in the nearest source domain.
Using this bandwidth, the bounds in Lemma
A.1
yield

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[d_{(1)}(u_{0})^{2\beta}\mid\mathcal{E}_{1}\right]\lesssim\left(\frac{K}{\gamma}\right)^{-2\beta},

\displaystyle p_{1}\asymp\left(1-(n\gamma^{2\beta})^{-\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}\right)_{+}^{K}\,,

which implies

r_{1}=\mathbb{E}\left[q_{1}^{2}{h}^{2\beta}+q_{2}S_{h}^{-1}\mid\mathcal{E}_{1}\right]\lesssim\left(\frac{K}{\gamma}\right)^{-2\beta}+\frac{1}{\bar{n}}\,.

Next, consider
\mathcal{E}_{2}
: We choose
h=d_{(K)}(u_{0})
in this case. This means all the domains are selected, and consequently, we have the following deterministic bound:

S_{h}=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{k=0}^{K}n_{k}\mathbf{1}\left\{|U_{k}-u_{0}|\leq h\right\}=\frac{n}{2}.

Another application of Lemma
A.1
yields:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[d_{(K)}(u_{0})^{2\beta}\mid\mathcal{E}_{2}\right]\lesssim\gamma^{2\beta},

\displaystyle p_{2}\coloneqq{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{E}_{2})\asymp 1-\left(1-(n\gamma^{2\beta})^{-\frac{K}{2\beta+1}}\right)_{+}\,.

Thus, it follows that

r_{2}=\mathbb{E}\left[q_{1}^{2}{h}^{2\beta}+q_{2}S_{h}^{-1}\mid\mathcal{E}_{2}\right]\lesssim\gamma^{2\beta}+\frac{1}{n}\,.

Finally, consider
\mathcal{E}_{3}
: In this case,
h=e_{0}(n/\gamma)^{-1/(2\beta+1)}
. Hence, the bias part is deterministically bounded by

\mathbb{E}\left[h^{2\beta}\mid\mathcal{E}_{3}\right]\lesssim\left(\frac{n}{\gamma}\right)^{-\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}}.

By Lemma
A.3
, the variance part is bounded by

\mathbb{E}\left[S_{h}^{-1}\mid\mathcal{E}_{3}\right]\lesssim\frac{\gamma}{nh}\asymp\left(\frac{n}{\gamma}\right)^{-\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}}\,.

As a consequence:

r_{3}=\mathbb{E}\left[q_{1}^{2}{h}^{2\beta}+q_{2}S_{h}^{-1}\mid\mathcal{E}_{j}\right]\lesssim\left(\frac{n}{\gamma}\right)^{-\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}},\qquad p_{3}=1-p_{1}-p_{2}\,.

Now that we have established bounds on
\{r_{j}\}
and
\{p_{j}\}
, we will bound the MSE using them. However, it is apparent from the definition of the events that one of these three events will dominate the others depending on the growth of
(n,\gamma,K)
.
We discuss the bounds for MSE under three circumstances:

i)\ n\gamma^{2\beta}\gg K^{2\beta+1},\qquad ii)\ n\gamma^{2\beta}\ll 1,\qquad iii)\ 1\lesssim n\gamma^{2\beta}\lesssim K^{2\beta+1}\,.

Case 1:
At first we consider the case
n\gamma^{2\beta}\gg K^{2\beta+1}
. Note that this implies:

n\gamma^{2\beta}\gg K^{2\beta+1}\begin{cases}\implies\bar{n}^{-1}\asymp(n/K)^{-1}\ll(\gamma/K)^{2\beta}\,,\\
\implies(n/\gamma)^{-2\beta/(2\beta+1)}\ll(\gamma/K)^{2\beta}\,.\end{cases}

Therefore, we have the upper bound on
r_{1}
:

r_{1}\lesssim\left(\frac{K}{\gamma}\right)^{-2\beta}+\frac{1}{\bar{n}}\lesssim\left(\frac{K}{\gamma}\right)^{-2\beta}\,,

and on
r_{3}
:

r_{3}\lesssim\left(\frac{n}{\gamma}\right)^{-\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}}\ll\left(\frac{K}{\gamma}\right)^{-2\beta}\,.

Furthermore, as
K\geq 1
, the assumption
n\gamma^{2\beta}\gg K^{2\beta+1}
immediately implies
n\gamma^{2\beta}\gg 1
, which, in turn, implies
n^{-1}\ll\gamma^{2\beta}
. Therefore, we have:

r_{2}\lesssim\gamma^{2\beta}+\frac{1}{n}\lesssim\gamma^{2\beta}.

Moreover, the same condition
n\gamma^{2\beta}\gg 1
also implies

\displaystyle p_{2}

\displaystyle\asymp 1-\big\{1-(n\gamma^{2\beta})^{-K/(2\beta+1)}\big\}_{+}\asymp(n\gamma^{2\beta})^{-K/(2\beta+1)}\,.

Combining all the bounds yields the following upper bound on the MSE of
\hat{\theta}_{\rm DVCM}
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\|\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\|_{A}^{2}\right]

\displaystyle\leq r_{1}p_{1}+r_{2}p_{2}+r_{3}(1-p_{1}-p_{2})

\displaystyle\leq r_{1}\vee r_{3}+r_{2}p_{2}

\displaystyle\lesssim\left(\frac{K}{\gamma}\right)^{-2\beta}+\gamma^{2\beta}(n\gamma^{2\beta})^{-\frac{K}{2\beta+1}}\lesssim\left(\frac{K}{\gamma}\right)^{-2\beta}\,.

Here, the last inequality follows from the fact that
(K/\gamma)^{-2\beta}\gg\gamma^{2\beta}(n\gamma^{2\beta})^{-K/(2\beta+1)}
as
n\gamma^{2\beta}\gg K^{2\beta+1}
. This completes the bound under Case 1.

Case 2:
In this case, we assume that
n\gamma^{2\beta}\ll 1\lesssim K^{2\beta+1}
, i.e.
\gamma^{2\beta}\ll n^{-1}
. This immediately implies:

r_{2}\lesssim\gamma^{2\beta}+\frac{1}{n}\lesssim\frac{1}{n}\,,

and

p_{2}\asymp 1-\big\{1-(n\gamma^{2\beta})^{-K/(2\beta+1)}\big\}_{+}\to 1\qquad\text{since }n\gamma^{2\beta}\downarrow 0\,,

and consequently
p_{1}=p_{3}=0
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\|\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\|_{A}^{2}\right]\lesssim r_{2}p_{2}\lesssim\frac{1}{n}.

Case 3:
Finally, in this case, we consider the last case,
1\lesssim n\gamma^{2\beta}\lesssim K^{2\beta+1}
.
We argue that in this case, MSE is
upper bounded by the rate
(n/\gamma)^{-2\beta/(2\beta+1)}
. To establish this, it is enough to show
r_{1}p_{1}\vee r_{2}p_{2}\lesssim(n/\gamma)^{-2\beta/(2\beta+1)}
, as
r_{3}p_{3}\lesssim(n/\gamma)^{-2\beta/(2\beta+1)}
by the bound on
r_{3}
.
With the definitions of
p_{1}
and
p_{2}
, it follows that

\displaystyle p_{1}\asymp\big(1-(n\gamma^{2\beta})^{-1/(2\beta+1)}\big)_{+}^{K}\lesssim{\sf exp}\left\{-K(n\gamma^{2\beta})^{-1/(2\beta+1)}\right\}\,,\qquad[\because(1-x)^{K}\lesssim e^{-Kx}\text{ for }x\in(0,1)]

\displaystyle p_{2}\asymp 1-\big\{1-(n\gamma^{2\beta})^{-K/(2\beta+1)}\big\}_{+}\lesssim(n\gamma^{2\beta})^{-K/(2\beta+1)}\,.\qquad[\because 1\lesssim n\gamma^{2\beta}]

As we are consider the scenario
1\lesssim n\gamma^{2\beta}\lesssim K^{2\beta+1}
, we have:

\displaystyle n\gamma^{2\beta}\lesssim K^{2\beta+1}

\displaystyle\implies\left(\frac{K}{\gamma}\right)^{-2\beta}\lesssim\frac{1}{\bar{n}}\,,

\displaystyle 1\lesssim n\gamma^{2\beta}

\displaystyle\implies\frac{1}{n}\lesssim\gamma^{2\beta}\,.

Thus we have the upper bounds for
r_{1}
and
r_{2}

\displaystyle r_{1}\lesssim\left(\frac{K}{\gamma}\right)^{-2\beta}+\frac{1}{\bar{n}}\lesssim\frac{1}{\bar{n}},

\displaystyle r_{2}\lesssim\gamma^{2\beta}+\frac{1}{n}\lesssim\gamma^{2\beta}\,.

These bounds, along with the upper bound on
(p_{1},p_{2})
, yield:

\displaystyle\frac{r_{1}p_{1}}{(n/\gamma)^{-\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}}}

\displaystyle=\frac{\bar{n}^{-1}{\sf exp}\left\{-K(n\gamma^{2\beta})^{-1/(2\beta+1)}\right\}}{(n/\gamma)^{-\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}}}

\displaystyle\asymp\frac{(K/n){\sf exp}\left\{-K(n\gamma^{2\beta})^{-1/(2\beta+1)}\right\}}{(n/\gamma)^{-\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}}}

\displaystyle=K(n\gamma^{2\beta})^{-1/(2\beta+1)}{\sf exp}\left\{-K(n\gamma^{2\beta})^{-1/(2\beta+1)}\right\}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1\,,\qquad[\because xe^{-x}\text{ is always upper bounded by a constant for }x>0]

and,

\displaystyle\frac{r_{2}p_{2}}{(n/\gamma)^{-\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}}}=\frac{\gamma^{2\beta}(n\gamma^{2\beta})^{-K/(2\beta+1)}}{(n/\gamma)^{-\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}}}

\displaystyle=(n\gamma^{2\beta})^{\frac{2\beta-K}{2\beta+1}}

\displaystyle\lesssim 1\qquad[\because n\gamma^{2\beta}\gtrsim 1,\text{ and assume }K\geq 2\beta\text{ w.l.o.g.}]

Hence, we have shown in this case that

\mathbb{E}\left[\|\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\|_{A}^{2}\right]\lesssim\left(\frac{n}{\gamma}\right)^{-\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}}\,.\

(B.2)

Justification of maximal upper bounds

As a short summary, we have established the following regime-specific upper bound on the mean squared error:

\begin{split}&\mathbb{E}\left[\|\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\|_{A}^{2}\right]\lesssim\begin{cases}(K/\gamma)^{-2\beta}&\text{if}\quad n\gamma^{2\beta}\gg K^{2\beta+1}\gtrsim 1,\\
(n/\gamma)^{-\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}}&\text{if}\quad 1\lesssim n\gamma^{2\beta}\lesssim K^{2\beta+1},\\
n^{-1}&\text{if}\quad n\gamma^{2\beta}\ll 1\lesssim K^{2\beta+1}\,.\end{cases}\end{split}

We next argue that, within each region, the overall upper bound is simply the maximum among the three individual upper bounds corresponding to that region, which follows from simple algebra. First observe that when
n\gamma^{2\beta}\gg K^{2\beta+1}
, then:

n\gamma^{2\beta}\gg K^{2\beta+1}\implies\frac{\gamma}{n}\ll\left(\frac{\gamma}{K}\right)^{2\beta+1}\implies\left(\frac{n}{\gamma}\right)^{-\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}}\ll\left(\frac{K}{\gamma}\right)^{-2\beta}\,,

and multiplying
K/\gamma
to middle inequality yields:

\frac{\gamma}{n}\ll\left(\frac{\gamma}{K}\right)^{2\beta+1}\implies\frac{1}{\bar{n}}\ll\left(\frac{K}{\gamma}\right)^{-2\beta}\implies\frac{1}{n}\ll\left(\frac{K}{\gamma}\right)^{-2\beta}\,.

Hence, we conclude:

n\gamma^{2\beta}\gg K^{2\beta+1}\implies\left(\frac{K}{\gamma}\right)^{-2\beta}\asymp\max\left\{\left(\frac{K}{\gamma}\right)^{-2\beta},\left(\frac{n}{\gamma}\right)^{-\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}},\frac{1}{n}\right\}.

Secondly, consider the case when
n\gamma^{2\beta}\ll 1
. In this case, we have:

\displaystyle\gamma^{2\beta}\ll n^{-1}\implies(\gamma/n)^{2\beta}\ll n^{-(2\beta+1)}\implies(n/\gamma)^{-\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}}\ll n^{-1}

and

\gamma^{2\beta}\ll n^{-1}\implies(\gamma/K)^{2\beta}\ll n^{-1}\,.

Hence,

n\gamma^{2\beta}\ll 1\implies\frac{1}{n}\asymp\max\left\{\left(\frac{K}{\gamma}\right)^{-2\beta},\left(\frac{n}{\gamma}\right)^{-\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}},\frac{1}{n}\right\}\,.

Finally, let us consider the third case
1\lesssim n\gamma^{2\beta}\lesssim K^{2\beta+1}
. In this case, we have:

n\gamma^{2\beta}\lesssim K^{2\beta+1}\implies(\gamma/K)^{2\beta+1}\lesssim\gamma/n\implies(K/\gamma)^{-2\beta}\lesssim(n/\gamma)^{-\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}}\,,

and

1\lesssim n\gamma^{2\beta}\implies n^{-1}\lesssim\gamma^{2\beta}\implies n^{-(2\beta+1)}\lesssim(\gamma/n)^{2\beta}\implies n^{-1}\lesssim(n/\gamma)^{-\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}}\,.

Therefore, in this case, we have:

1\lesssim n\gamma^{2\beta}\lesssim K^{2\beta+1}\implies\left(\frac{n}{\gamma}\right)^{-\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}}\asymp\max\left\{\left(\frac{K}{\gamma}\right)^{-2\beta},\left(\frac{n}{\gamma}\right)^{-\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}},\frac{1}{n}\right\}\,.

This completes the proof.

B.2

Proof of Theorem
3.6

Proof B.2

We apply Theorem 1 of
[
41
]
, which roughly says that for any two estimators
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{1}
and
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{2}
,
M(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{1})\succeq M(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{2})\implies\mathrm{MSE}_{A}(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{1})\geq\mathrm{MSE}_{A}(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{2})
for any
A\succeq 0
.
Our goal is to show that there exists a matrix
Q
that makes the difference between squared error matrices positive semidefinite, i.e.

M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)-M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})\right)\succeq 0\,,

and

M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{LR}}(u_{0})\right)-M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})\right)\succeq 0\,.

Define
S_{Q}=\hat{\Sigma}_{0}+Q
with
\hat{\Sigma}_{0}=(\mathbf{X}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{0})/n_{0}
and
\mathbf{X}_{0}
is a matrix concatenating all the
\{X_{0i}\}_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{0}^{*}}
. From the first order condition, we have:

\displaystyle\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})

\displaystyle=\left(\frac{1}{n_{0}}(\mathbf{X}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{X}_{0})+Q\right)^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{n_{0}}(\mathbf{X}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{0})+Q\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)

\displaystyle=(\hat{\Sigma}_{0}+Q)^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{n_{0}}(\mathbf{X}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{0})+Q\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)

\displaystyle=S_{Q}^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{n_{0}}(\mathbf{X}_{0}^{\top}\mathbf{y}_{0})+Q\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)

\displaystyle=S_{Q}^{-1}\left(\hat{\Sigma}_{0}{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})+\frac{1}{n_{0}}\mathbf{X}_{0}^{\top}{\bm{\epsilon}}_{0}+Q\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)\qquad[\because\mathbf{y}_{0}=\mathbf{X}_{0}{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})+{\bm{\epsilon}}_{0}]

\displaystyle=S_{Q}^{-1}\left((S_{Q}-Q){\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})+\frac{1}{n_{0}}\mathbf{X}_{0}^{\top}{\bm{\epsilon}}_{0}+Q\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)

\displaystyle={\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})+S_{Q}^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{n_{0}}\mathbf{X}_{0}^{\top}{\bm{\epsilon}}_{0}\right)+S_{Q}^{-1}Q(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0}))\,.

Therefore, the conditional bias of
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})
is

\begin{split}{\sf Bias}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})\middle|\mathbf{X}_{0}\right)=S_{Q}^{-1}Q{\sf Bias}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\middle|\mathbf{X}_{0}\right)=S_{Q}^{-1}Q{\sf Bias}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)\end{split}

because
\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})
is independent of
\mathbf{X}_{0}
due to sample splitting and
\mathbb{E}[{\epsilon}_{0}\mid\mathbf{X}_{0}]=0
on the target domain. Now for the conditional variance, we utilize this independence again and have

\begin{split}&{\sf Var}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})\middle|\mathbf{X}_{0}\right)\\
&={\sf Var}\left[S_{Q}^{-1}\left(\frac{1}{n_{0}}\mathbf{X}_{0}^{\top}{\bm{\epsilon}}_{0}+Q(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0}))\right)\middle|\mathbf{X}_{0}\right]\\
&=S_{Q}^{-1}\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}(u_{0})}{n_{0}}\hat{\Sigma}_{0}+Q{\sf Var}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)Q\right)S_{Q}^{-1}.\end{split}

Hence,

\begin{split}M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})\middle|\mathbf{X}_{0}\right)&={\sf Bias}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})\middle|\mathbf{X}_{0}\right)^{\otimes 2}+{\sf Var}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})\middle|\mathbf{X}_{0}\right)\\
&=S_{Q}^{-1}\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}(u_{0})}{n_{0}}\hat{\Sigma}_{0}+QM\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)Q\right)S_{Q}^{-1}\,.\end{split}

Using the fact that

\displaystyle M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{LR}}(u_{0})\middle|\mathbf{X}_{0}\right)=\frac{\sigma^{2}(u_{0})}{n_{0}}\hat{\Sigma}^{-1}_{0}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma^{2}(u_{0})}{n_{0}}S_{Q}^{-1}S_{Q}\hat{\Sigma}^{-1}_{0}S_{Q}S_{Q}^{-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sigma^{2}(u_{0})}{n_{0}}S_{Q}^{-1}\left[\hat{\Sigma}_{0}+2Q+Q\hat{\Sigma}^{-1}_{0}Q\right]S_{Q}^{-1}\,,\qquad[\because S_{Q}=\hat{\Sigma}_{0}+Q]

we obtain the difference between the second-order moments

\begin{split}&M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{LR}}(u_{0})\right)-M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})\right)\\
=&\mathbb{E}\left[M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{LR}}(u_{0})\middle|\mathbf{X}_{0}\right)-M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})\middle|\mathbf{X}_{0}\right)\right]\\
=&\mathbb{E}\left[S_{Q}^{-1}Q\left[\frac{\sigma^{2}(u_{0})}{n_{0}}\hat{\Sigma}^{-1}_{0}-M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)+\frac{2\sigma^{2}(u_{0})}{n_{0}}Q^{-1}\right]QS_{Q}^{-1}\right]\end{split}

and

\begin{split}&M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)-M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})\right)\\
=&\mathbb{E}\left[M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\middle|\mathbf{X}_{0}\right)-M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})\middle|\mathbf{X}_{0}\right)\right]\\
=&\mathbb{E}\left[M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)-S_{Q}^{-1}\left(\frac{\sigma^{2}(u_{0})}{n_{0}}\hat{\Sigma}_{0}+QM\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)Q\right)S_{Q}^{-1}\right]\\
=&\mathbb{E}\left\{S_{Q}^{-1}\left(S_{Q}M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)S_{Q}-\frac{\sigma^{2}(u_{0})}{n_{0}}\hat{\Sigma}_{0}-QM\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)Q\right)S_{Q}^{-1}\right\}.\end{split}

Replacing
S_{Q}
with
\hat{\Sigma}_{0}+Q
, we have

\begin{split}&M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)-M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})\right)\\
=&\mathbb{E}\bigg\{S_{Q}^{-1}\hat{\Sigma}_{0}\left[M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)-\frac{\sigma^{2}(u_{0})}{n_{0}}\hat{\Sigma}^{-1}_{0}+\hat{\Sigma}^{-1}_{0}QM\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)\right.\\
&\left.\hskip 200.0003pt+M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)Q\hat{\Sigma}^{-1}_{0}\right]\hat{\Sigma}_{0}S_{Q}^{-1}\bigg\}\,.\end{split}

Notice that if we set
Q
such that

\frac{1}{2}\frac{\sigma^{2}(u_{0})}{n_{0}}M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)^{-1}\preceq Q\preceq 2\frac{\sigma^{2}(u_{0})}{n_{0}}M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)^{-1}

then due to the positive semidefiniteness of
M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)
,
\hat{\Sigma}_{0}
, and
S_{Q}^{-1}
, the following holds

\begin{split}&M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{LR}}(u_{0})\right)-M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})\right)\succeq 0,\\
&M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)-M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})\right)\succeq 0.\end{split}

By Theorem 1 of
[
41
]
, this implies that

\begin{split}\mathrm{MSE}_{A}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})\right)&\leq\min\left\{\mathrm{MSE}_{A}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right),\mathrm{MSE}_{A}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{LR}}(u_{0})\right)\right\}\,,\end{split}

for any positive semidefinite
A
.

Then proof of the theorem follows from Proposition
3.5
, and
we have already argued that
\mathrm{MSE}_{A}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{LR}}(u_{0})\right)\leq Cn_{0}^{-1}
, which follows from basic properties of linear regression. Furthermore, in Proposition
3.5
we have established that:

\mathrm{MSE}_{A}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)\leq C\max\left\{(K/\gamma)^{-2\beta},(n/\gamma)^{-\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}},n^{-1}\right\}\,.

Hence, combining these, we obtain:

\displaystyle\mathrm{MSE}_{A}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL}}(u_{0})\right)

\displaystyle\leq\min\left\{\mathrm{MSE}_{A}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{LR}}(u_{0})\right),\ \mathrm{MSE}_{A}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\leq C\left[n_{0}^{-1}\wedge\max\left((K/\gamma)^{-2\beta},(n/\gamma)^{-\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}},n^{-1}\right)\right]\,.

This completes the proof.

B.3

Proof of Corollary
3.8

Proof B.3

Define the event

\mathcal{E}=\left\{\frac{1}{2}\frac{\sigma^{2}(u_{0})}{n_{0}}M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)^{-1}\preceq\hat{Q}\preceq 2\frac{\sigma^{2}(u_{0})}{n_{0}}M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)^{-1}\right\}\,,

and the desired rate

r_{n,K,\gamma}=\sqrt{n_{0}\vee\min\left\{(K/\gamma)^{2\beta},\ (n/\gamma)^{2\beta/(2\beta+1)},n\right\}}\,.

By Markov’s inequality, it follows that

\begin{split}{\mathbb{P}}\left(r_{n,K,\gamma}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL},\hat{Q}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\right)\geq t\right)&={\mathbb{P}}\left(r_{n,K,\gamma}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL},\hat{Q}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\right)\geq t,\mathcal{E}\right)+{\mathbb{P}}\left(r_{n,K,\gamma}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL},\hat{Q}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\right)\geq t,\mathcal{E}^{c}\right)\\
&\leq{\mathbb{P}}\left(r_{n,K,\gamma}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL},\hat{Q}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\right)\geq t\middle|\mathcal{E}\right)+{\mathbb{P}}\left(\mathcal{E}^{c}\right)\\
&\leq\frac{r_{n,K,\gamma}^{2}}{t^{2}}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL},\hat{Q}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\right)^{2}\middle|\mathcal{E}\right]+{\mathbb{P}}\left(\mathcal{E}^{c}\right)\end{split}

Consider the expectation term. By Theorem
3.6
, it holds that

r_{n,K,\gamma}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL},\hat{Q}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\right)^{2}\middle|\mathcal{E}\right]\leq C

for some constant
C>0
, and thus we have

{\mathbb{P}}\left(r_{n,K,\gamma}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL},\hat{Q}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\right)\geq t\right)\leq\frac{C}{t^{2}}+{\mathbb{P}}\left(\mathcal{E}^{c}\right)\,.

Taking
\limsup_{n\to\infty}
on both sides, it follows that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}{\mathbb{P}}\left(r_{n,K,\gamma}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL},\hat{Q}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\right)\geq t\right)\leq\frac{C}{t^{2}}+\limsup_{n\to\infty}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\mathcal{E}^{c}\right)\,.

Now let’s deal with term
{\mathbb{P}}\left(\mathcal{E}^{c}\right)
. By definition of
\mathcal{E}
, we obtain

\begin{split}&{\mathbb{P}}\left(\mathcal{E}\right)\\
&={\mathbb{P}}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}\frac{\sigma^{2}(u_{0})}{n_{0}}M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)^{-1}\preceq\delta\frac{\hat{\sigma}^{2}(u_{0})}{n_{0}}\hat{M}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)^{-1}\preceq 2\frac{\sigma^{2}(u_{0})}{n_{0}}M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)^{-1}\bigg)\\
&={\mathbb{P}}\bigg(\frac{1}{2}I\preceq\delta\frac{\hat{\sigma}^{2}(u_{0})}{\sigma^{2}(u_{0})}M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)\hat{M}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)^{-1}\preceq 2I\bigg)\,.\end{split}

By their definitions,

\displaystyle\frac{\hat{\sigma}^{2}(u_{0})}{\sigma^{2}(u_{0})}=1+o_{p}(1)

\displaystyle M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)\hat{M}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)^{-1}=I+o_{p}(1)

and thus the middle term

\delta\frac{\hat{\sigma}^{2}(u_{0})}{\sigma^{2}(u_{0})}M\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)\hat{M}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{DVCM}}(u_{0})\right)^{-1}\xrightarrow[p]{n\to\infty}\delta I\,.

The coefficient
\delta\in(\frac{1}{2},2)
by its definition, and this implies that as
n\to\infty
,

{\mathbb{P}}(\mathcal{E}^{c})\rightarrow 0.

Hence, we get

\limsup_{n\to\infty}{\mathbb{P}}\left(r_{n,K,\gamma}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL},\hat{Q}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\right)\geq t\right)\leq\frac{C}{t^{2}}\,,

which means that

r_{n,K,\gamma}\left(\hat{\bm{\theta}}_{\mathrm{TL},\hat{Q}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\right)=O_{p}(1)\qquad\text{as }n\to\infty.

B.4

Proof of Theorem
3.9

Proof B.4

We want to establish a lower bound for

\inf_{\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(u_{0})}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\forall j\in[p],\\
\theta_{j}\in\mathcal{H}(\beta,L)\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\left(\|\hat{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\|_{A}^{2}\right).

We will use Le Cam’s approach
[
44
]
to find the minimax lower bound. We consider the data generating process

Y_{ki}|U_{k},X_{ki}\sim\mathcal{N}(X_{ki}^{\top}{\bm{\theta}}_{0}(U_{k}),1),\quad{\bm{\theta}}_{0}=(\theta_{01},\ldots,\theta_{0p})^{\top};

Y_{ki}|U_{k},X_{ki}\sim\mathcal{N}(X_{ki}^{\top}{\bm{\theta}}_{1}(U_{k}),1),\quad{\bm{\theta}}_{1}=(\theta_{11},\ldots,\theta_{1p})^{\top}.

Recall that
A
is a positive semidefinite matrix whose
j
-th largest eigenvalue is
\lambda_{A,j}
(with
\lambda_{A,1}>0
) and corresponding eigenvector is
\mathbf{v}_{A,j}=[v_{A,j1},\ldots,v_{A,jp}]^{\top}
, with which each coordinate of functional coefficients is defined as

\displaystyle\theta_{0j}(u)

\displaystyle\equiv 0

\displaystyle\text{ for }j=1,\ldots,p

\displaystyle\theta_{1j}(u)

\displaystyle=v_{A,1j}Lh^{\beta}W\left(\frac{u-u_{0}}{h}\right)

\displaystyle\text{ for }j=1,\ldots,p,

where
W(u)=c_{0}{\sf exp}\left(-\frac{1}{1-u^{2}}\right)\mathbf{1}(|u|\leq 1)
, and
c_{0}>0
is a small constant. There exists a threshold
C_{0}>0
such that if
c_{0}\leq C_{0}
, then
W
belongs to
\mathcal{H}(\beta,1/2)
.
Using the definition
l=\lfloor\beta\rfloor
and the fact that
W\in\mathcal{H}(\beta,1/2)
, it is shown that

\left|\theta_{1j}^{(l)}(u)-\theta_{1j}^{(l)}(u^{\prime})\right|=|v_{A,1j}|Lh^{\beta-l}\left|W^{(l)}\left(\frac{u-u_{0}}{h}\right)-W^{(l)}\left(\frac{u^{\prime}-u_{0}}{h}\right)\right|\leq\frac{|v_{A,1j}|L}{2}|u-u^{\prime}|^{\beta-l},

thus the constructed hypotheses lie in the parameter space. That is,
\forall j\in[p],\theta_{1j}\in\mathcal{H}(\beta,L)
. Moreover, we select domain sizes to be equal:

n_{k}\equiv\bar{n}\qquad\text{for }k=0,1,\ldots,K,

(B.3)

let
U_{k}
’s be iid from pdf

\frac{1}{\gamma}f\left(\frac{u-u^{*}}{\gamma}\right),\qquad\text{with }f(\cdot)\leq a_{0},

(B.4)

and make
X_{ki}
bounded:

\|X_{ki}\|_{2}\leq 1\,.

(B.5)

Such choices of
n_{k},U_{k}
and
X_{ki}
also lie in the presumed DGP, see Assumptions
3.2
and
3.3
. Our metric of interest is lower bounded by

\displaystyle\left\|{\bm{\theta}}_{1}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}_{0}(u_{0})\right\|_{A}=\sqrt{{\bm{\theta}}_{1}(u_{0})^{\top}A{\bm{\theta}}_{1}(u_{0})}

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\sum_{j=1}^{p}\lambda_{A,j}\left(\mathbf{v}_{A,j}^{\top}{\bm{\theta}}_{1}(u_{0})\right)^{2}}

\displaystyle\geq\sqrt{\lambda_{A,1}\left(\mathbf{v}_{A,1}^{\top}{\bm{\theta}}_{1}(u_{0})\right)^{2}}=\sqrt{\lambda_{A,1}}Lh^{\beta}W(0)\,.

We set

r_{n,K,\gamma}=\sqrt{\lambda_{A,1}}Lh^{\beta}W(0)/3\,,

with
h
to be chosen later.
Let

{\mathbb{P}}_{0}
and
{\mathbb{P}}_{1}
are the joint distributions of
\{(U_{k},X_{ki},Y_{ki}):k\in\{0\}\cup[K],i\in[n_{k}]\}
under hypotheses
{\bm{\theta}}_{0}
and
{\bm{\theta}}_{1}
. Then the following holds:

\begin{split}\inf_{\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(u_{0})}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\forall j\in[p],\\
\theta_{j}\in\mathcal{H}(\beta,L)\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\left(\|\hat{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\|_{A}^{2}\right)&\geq\inf_{\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(u_{0})}\max_{{\bm{\theta}}\in\{{\bm{\theta}}_{0},{\bm{\theta}}_{1}\}}\mathbb{E}\left(\|\hat{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\|_{A}^{2}\right)\\
&\geq r_{n,K,\gamma}^{2}\inf_{\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(u_{0})}\max_{{\bm{\theta}}\in\{{\bm{\theta}}_{0},{\bm{\theta}}_{1}\}}{\mathbb{P}}\left(\|\hat{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\|_{A}\geq r_{n,K,\gamma}\right)\\
&\geq r_{n,K,\gamma}^{2}\inf_{\psi}\max_{j\in\{0,1\}}{\mathbb{P}}_{j}\left(\psi\neq j\right),\end{split}

(B.6)

Now we justify the last inequality in (
B.6
). By definition of
r_{n,K,\gamma}
, it holds that

\|{\bm{\theta}}_{1}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}_{0}(u_{0})\|_{A}>2r_{n,K,\gamma}\,.

Then, for any estimator
\hat{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})
, it is impossible for both

\|\hat{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}_{0}(u_{0})\|_{A}<r_{n,K,\gamma}\quad\text{and}\quad\|\hat{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}_{1}(u_{0})\|_{A}<r_{n,K,\gamma}

to simultaneously hold. Indeed, by the triangle inequality, this would imply

\|{\bm{\theta}}_{1}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}_{0}(u_{0})\|_{A}\leq\|{\bm{\theta}}_{1}(u_{0})-\hat{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\|_{A}+\|\hat{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}_{0}(u_{0})\|_{A}<2r_{n,K,\gamma},

which contradicts the assumption. Therefore, for any estimator
\hat{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})
, at least one of the following events must occur:

\|\hat{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}_{0}(u_{0})\|_{A}\geq r_{n,K,\gamma}\quad\text{or}\quad\|\hat{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}_{1}(u_{0})\|_{A}\geq r_{n,K,\gamma}.

Now define a test function
\psi\in\{0,1\}
based on the estimator by

\psi=\argmin_{j\in\{0,1\}}\|\hat{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}_{j}(u_{0})\|_{A}\,,

which selects the hypothesis closest to the estimator. Then,

\psi\neq j\quad\implies\quad\|\hat{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}_{j}(u_{0})\|_{A}\geq r_{n,K,\gamma}\,,

so that

\max_{j\in\{0,1\}}{\mathbb{P}}_{j}\left(\|\hat{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}_{j}(u_{0})\|_{A}\geq r_{n,K,\gamma}\right)\geq\max_{j\in\{0,1\}}{\mathbb{P}}_{j}(\psi\neq j),

which proves last line of (
B.6
). Moreover, by the definition of total variation distance and Pinsker’s inequality, we have

\begin{split}{\mathbb{P}}_{0}(\psi\neq 0)+{\mathbb{P}}_{1}(\psi\neq 1)&=1-\left({\mathbb{P}}_{0}(\psi\neq 1)-{\mathbb{P}}_{1}(\psi\neq 1)\right)\\
&\geq 1-{\sf TV}({\mathbb{P}}_{0},{\mathbb{P}}_{1})\\
&\geq 1-\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}{\sf KL}\left({\mathbb{P}}_{0}\ \|\ {\mathbb{P}}_{1}\right)},\end{split}

(B.7)

and thus

\inf_{\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(u_{0})}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\forall j\in[p],\\
\theta_{j}\in\mathcal{H}(\beta,L)\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\left(\|\hat{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\|_{A}^{2}\right)\geq\frac{r_{n,K,\gamma}^{2}}{2}\left(1-\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}{\sf KL}\left({\mathbb{P}}_{0}\ \|\ {\mathbb{P}}_{1}\right)}\right)\,.

(B.8)

This completes the reduction part in Le Cam’s approach. Now let
p_{0},p_{1}
be the density functions associated with
{\mathbb{P}}_{0},{\mathbb{P}}_{1}
, then

\begin{split}&{\sf KL}({\mathbb{P}}_{0}\ \|\ {\mathbb{P}}_{1})\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{0}}\left[\log\frac{p_{0}\left(U_{k},X_{ki}:k\in\{0\}\cup[K],i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}\right)\prod_{k=0}^{K}\prod_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}p_{0}\left(Y_{ki}\mid U_{k},X_{ki}\right)}{p_{1}\left(U_{k},X_{ki}:k\in\{0\}\cup[K],i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}\right)\prod_{k=0}^{K}\prod_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}p_{1}\left(Y_{ki}\mid U_{k},X_{ki}\right)}\right],\end{split}

where
p_{j}\left(U_{k},X_{ki}:k\in\{0\}\cup[K],i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}\right)
is the joint density function of all the
U_{k}
and
X_{ki}
, and
p_{j}\left(Y_{ki}\mid U_{k},X_{ki}\right)
is the conditional density of
Y_{ki}
given
(U_{k},X_{ki})
. The marginal distribution of
U_{k},X_{ki}
are the same under the two hypotheses, which implies

p_{0}\left(U_{k},X_{ki}:k\in\{0\}\cup[K],i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}\right)=p_{1}\left(U_{k},X_{ki}:k\in\{0\}\cup[K],i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}\right),

so it suffices to consider the conditional distribution
Y_{ki}|U_{k},X_{i}
when calculating
{\sf KL}({\mathbb{P}}_{0}\ \|\ {\mathbb{P}}_{1})
. Utilizing the normality of
Y_{ki}|U_{k},X_{ki}
and letting
\varphi
be the pdf of standard normal distribution, we obtain:

\begin{split}{\sf KL}({\mathbb{P}}_{0}\ \|\ {\mathbb{P}}_{1})&=\mathbb{E}_{{\mathbb{P}}_{0}}\left[\log\frac{\prod_{k=0}^{K}\prod_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}p_{0}\left(Y_{ki}\mid U_{k},X_{ki}\right)}{\prod_{k=0}^{K}\prod_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}p_{1}\left(Y_{ki}\mid U_{k},X_{ki}\right)}\right]\\
&=\sum_{k=0}^{K}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}\mathbb{E}_{U_{k},X_{ki}}\int\log\frac{\varphi(t)}{\varphi\left(t-{\bm{\theta}}_{1}(U_{k})\cdot X_{ki}\right)}\varphi(t)dt\\
&=\sum_{k=0}^{K}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}\mathbb{E}_{U_{k},X_{ki}}\left({\bm{\theta}}_{1}(U_{k})\cdot X_{ki}\right)^{2}\\
&\leq\sum_{k=0}^{K}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}\mathbb{E}_{U_{k}}\|{\bm{\theta}}_{1}(U_{k})\|^{2}_{2}.\quad[\because\text{Equation \eqref{eq:minimax-lb-cond-X}}]\end{split}

(B.9)

It holds by definition of
{\bm{\theta}}_{1}
that
\|{\bm{\theta}}_{1}(U_{k})\|^{2}_{2}=L^{2}h^{2\beta}W^{2}\left(\frac{u_{0}-U_{k}}{h}\right)\leq L^{2}h^{2\beta}W^{2}\left(0\right)\mathbf{1}\left(\left|u_{0}-U_{k}\right|\leq h\right)
, so we get an upper bound:

\begin{split}{\sf KL}({\mathbb{P}}_{0}\ \|\ {\mathbb{P}}_{1})&\leq\sum_{k=0}^{K}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}\mathbb{E}_{U_{k}}\left[\|{\bm{\theta}}_{1}(U_{k})\|^{2}_{2}\right]\\
&\leq L^{2}W^{2}\left(0\right)h^{2\beta}\sum_{k=0}^{K}\sum_{i\in\mathcal{I}_{k}}\mathbb{E}_{U_{k}}\left[\mathbf{1}\left(\left|u_{0}-U_{k}\right|\leq h\right)\right]\\
&\leq L^{2}c_{0}^{2}h^{2\beta}\sum_{k=0}^{K}n_{k}\mathbb{E}_{U_{k}}\left[\mathbf{1}\left(\left|u_{0}-U_{k}\right|\leq h\right)\right].\quad[\because W(0)\leq c_{0}\text{ by its definition}]\end{split}

(B.10)

Due to Equation (
B.4
) on the upper bound of density of
U_{k}
, for any
k\in[K]
, the expectation part is upper bounded by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{U_{k}}\left[\mathbf{1}\left(\left|u_{0}-U_{k}\right|\leq h\right)\right]\leq\frac{2a_{0}h}{\gamma}\,,

while for
k=0
,

\mathbb{E}_{U_{k}}\left[\mathbf{1}\left(\left|u_{0}-U_{k}\right|\leq h\right)\right]=1\,,

and thus

\displaystyle{\sf KL}({\mathbb{P}}_{0}\ \|\ {\mathbb{P}}_{1})\leq L^{2}c_{0}^{2}h^{2\beta}\left(n_{0}+\frac{2a_{0}h}{\gamma}\sum_{k=1}^{K}n_{k}\right)\leq(1\vee 2a_{0})L^{2}c_{0}^{2}h^{2\beta}\left(n_{0}+\frac{h}{\gamma}\sum_{k=1}^{K}n_{k}\right)\,.

Because of the inequality
x+y\leq 2(x\vee y)
for
x,y>0
, we have

{\sf KL}({\mathbb{P}}_{0}\ \|\ {\mathbb{P}}_{1})\leq(2\vee 4a_{0})L^{2}c_{0}^{2}h^{2\beta}\left(n_{0}\vee\frac{h}{\gamma}\sum_{k=1}^{K}n_{k}\right)\,.

(B.11)

We are going to discuss 3 possible choices of
h\in\{h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}\}
. All the choices of
h
lead to the same upper bound of KL-divergence, that is
{\sf KL}({\mathbb{P}}_{0}\ \|\ {\mathbb{P}}_{1})\leq 2\alpha
for some constant
\alpha\in(0,1/2)
. Then we apply Equation (
B.8
) and use the definition
r_{n,K,\gamma}=\sqrt{\lambda_{A,1}}Lh^{\beta}W(0)/3
to obtain

\begin{split}\inf_{\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(u_{0})}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\forall j\in[p],\\
\theta_{j}\in\mathcal{H}(\beta,L)\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\left(\|\hat{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\|_{A}^{2}\right)&\geq\frac{r_{n,K,\gamma}^{2}}{2}\left(1-\sqrt{\frac{1}{2}{\sf KL}\left({\mathbb{P}}_{0}\ \|\ {\mathbb{P}}_{1}\right)}\right)\\
&\geq\frac{\Big\{\sqrt{\lambda_{A,1}}Lh_{j}^{\beta}W(0)/3\Big\}^{2}}{2}\left(1-\sqrt{\alpha}\right)\quad\text{for }j=1,2,3\\
&=\frac{\lambda_{A,1}L^{2}W^{2}(0)h_{j}^{2\beta}}{18}\left(1-\sqrt{\alpha}\right)\quad\text{for }j=1,2,3.\end{split}

(B.12)

Hence, we are able to take maximum across the 3
h_{j}
’s to find a tighter bound, and this yields the lower bound

\inf_{\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(u_{0})}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\forall j\in[p],\\
\theta_{j}\in\mathcal{H}(\beta,L)\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\left(\|\hat{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\|_{A}^{2}\right)\geq\max_{j\in\{1,2,3\}}\frac{\lambda_{A,1}L^{2}W^{2}(0)h_{j}^{2\beta}}{18}\left(1-\sqrt{\alpha}\right)\,.

Now, we discuss the 3 choices of
h\in\{h_{1},h_{2},h_{3}\}
, and their corresponding minimax lower bound.

Case 1:
In this case, we find the first choice of
h
:

h=h_{1}=n_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2\beta}}\wedge\left(\gamma/K\right),

and use it to find corresponding
\alpha
, and upper bound of KL-divergence. Equation (
B.3
) says
n_{k}\equiv\bar{n}
for
k=0,1,\ldots,K
. Following the intermediate result in Equation (
B.11
) we then obtain an upper bound

{\sf KL}({\mathbb{P}}_{0}\ \|\ {\mathbb{P}}_{1})\leq(2\vee 4a_{0})L^{2}c_{0}^{2}h^{2\beta}\bar{n}\left(1\vee\frac{hK}{\gamma}\right)\,.

With the definition of
h=h_{1}
, it follows that

\displaystyle 1\vee\frac{hK}{\gamma}\leq 1\vee\frac{K}{\gamma}\left(n_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2\beta}}\wedge\left(\gamma/K\right)\right)\leq 1\,.

Also, by Equation (
B.3
),
n_{0}=\bar{n}
, and we obtain

h^{2\beta}=\left(n_{0}^{-1}\wedge\left(K/\gamma\right)^{-2\beta}\right)\leq n_{0}^{-1}=\bar{n}^{-1}\,.

Hence, we can plug the upper bound for
1\vee hK/\gamma
and
h^{2\beta}
into
{\sf KL}({\mathbb{P}}_{0}\ \|\ {\mathbb{P}}_{1})
’s upper bound. This yields

{\sf KL}({\mathbb{P}}_{0}\ \|\ {\mathbb{P}}_{1})\leq(2\vee 4a_{0})L^{2}c_{0}^{2}\,.

Note that the rate of
r_{n,K,\gamma}=\sqrt{\lambda_{A,1}}Lh^{\beta}W(0)/3
is immediately obtained from the selected
h
in each case. Therefore, by Equation (
B.8
) we have

\begin{split}\inf_{\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(u_{0})}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\forall j\in[p],\\
\theta_{j}\in\mathcal{H}(\beta,L)\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\left(\|\hat{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\|_{A}^{2}\right)&\geq\frac{r_{n,K,\gamma}^{2}}{2}\left(1-\sqrt{(1\vee 2a_{0})L^{2}c_{0}^{2}}\right)\\
&=\frac{\lambda_{A,1}L^{2}W^{2}(0)\left(n_{0}^{-1}\wedge\left(K/\gamma\right)^{-2\beta}\right)}{18}\left(1-\sqrt{(1\vee 2a_{0})L^{2}c_{0}^{2}}\right)\\
&=\frac{\lambda_{A,1}L^{2}c_{0}^{2}\left(n_{0}^{-1}\wedge\left(K/\gamma\right)^{-2\beta}\right)}{18e^{2}}\left(1-\sqrt{(1\vee 2a_{0})L^{2}c_{0}^{2}}\right)\end{split}

(B.13)

where the last line is by definition

W(u)=c_{0}{\sf exp}\left(-\frac{1}{1-u^{2}}\right)\mathbf{1}(|u|\leq 1)\implies W(0)=\frac{c_{0}}{e}.

We want to find
c_{0}
such that

1-\sqrt{(1\vee 2a_{0})L^{2}c_{0}^{2}}\geq\frac{1}{2}\implies c_{0}\leq\frac{1}{2\sqrt{(1\vee 2a_{0})L^{2}}}\,.

Recall that
W\in\mathcal{H}(\beta,1/2)
only if
c_{0}\leq C_{0}
, so we can set

c_{0}=C_{0}\wedge\frac{1}{2\sqrt{(1\vee 2a_{0})L^{2}}}\,.

This yields a lower bound

\inf_{\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(u_{0})}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\forall j\in[p],\\
\theta_{j}\in\mathcal{H}(\beta,L)\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\left(\|\hat{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\|_{A}^{2}\right)\geq\frac{\lambda_{A,1}L^{2}c_{0}^{2}}{36e^{2}}\left(n_{0}^{-1}\wedge\left(K/\gamma\right)^{-2\beta}\right).

Case 2:
In this case, we define

h=h_{2}=n_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2\beta}}\wedge n^{-\frac{1}{2\beta}}\implies h^{2\beta}=n_{0}^{-1}\wedge n^{-1}\,.

Plugging this bandwidth into Equation (
B.10
) yields

\begin{split}{\sf KL}({\mathbb{P}}_{0}\ \|\ {\mathbb{P}}_{1})&\leq L^{2}c_{0}^{2}h^{2\beta}\mathbb{E}_{U_{k}}\left[\sum_{k=0}^{K}n_{k}\mathbf{1}\left(\left|u_{0}-U_{k}\right|\leq h\right)\right].\end{split}

Notice that the following upper bound always holds:

\sum_{k=0}^{K}n_{k}\mathbf{1}\left(\left|u_{0}-U_{k}\right|\leq h\right)\leq n.

Hence,

\begin{split}{\sf KL}({\mathbb{P}}_{0}\ \|\ {\mathbb{P}}_{1})&\leq L^{2}c_{0}^{2}h^{2\beta}n\\
&=L^{2}c_{0}^{2}\left(n_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2\beta}}\wedge n^{-\frac{1}{2\beta}}\right)^{2\beta}n\\
&\leq L^{2}c_{0}^{2}\\
&\leq(2\vee 4a_{0})L^{2}c_{0}^{2}\,.\end{split}

Again, by Equation (
B.8
) we have

\begin{split}\inf_{\hat{{\bm{\theta}}}(u_{0})}\sup_{\begin{subarray}{c}\forall j\in[p],\\
\theta_{j}\in\mathcal{H}(\beta,L)\end{subarray}}\mathbb{E}\left(\|\hat{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})-{\bm{\theta}}(u_{0})\|_{A}^{2}\right)&\geq\frac{r_{n,K,\gamma}^{2}}{2}\left(1-\sqrt{(1\vee 2a_{0})L^{2}c_{0}^{2}}\right)\\
&=\frac{\lambda_{A,1}L^{2}c_{0}^{2}\left(n_{0}^{-1}\wedge n^{-1}\right)}{18e^{2}}\left(1-\sqrt{(1\vee 2a_{0})L^{2}c_{0}^{2}}\right)\end{split}

(B.14)

Same as Case 1, we can set
c_{0}=C_{0}\wedge\frac{1}{2\sqrt{(1\vee 2a_{0})L^{2}}}
to find lower bound

\geq\frac{\lambda_{A,1}L^{2}c_{0}^{2}}{36e^{2}}\left(n_{0}^{-1}\wedge n^{-1}\right)\,.

Case 3:
In this case, we utilize the third choice of
h
:

h=h_{3}=(n/\gamma)^{-\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}\wedge n_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2\beta}}.

Firstly, it follows from Equation (
B.11
) that

\begin{split}{\sf KL}({\mathbb{P}}_{0}\ \|\ {\mathbb{P}}_{1})&\leq(2\vee 4a_{0})L^{2}c_{0}^{2}h^{2\beta}\left(n_{0}\vee\frac{h}{\gamma}\sum_{k=1}^{K}n_{k}\right)\,.\end{split}

By the construction of
h=h_{3}
we have

\displaystyle n_{0}\vee\frac{h}{\gamma}\sum_{k=1}^{K}n_{k}=n_{0}\vee\frac{(n/\gamma)^{-\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}\wedge n_{0}^{-\frac{1}{2\beta}}}{\gamma}\sum_{k=1}^{K}n_{k}\leq n_{0}\vee\frac{(n/\gamma)^{-\frac{1}{2\beta+1}}}{\gamma}\sum_{k=0}^{K}n_{k}\,.

Recall that
n=\sum_{k=0}^{K}n_{k}
and thus

n_{0}\vee\frac{h}{\gamma}\sum_{k=1}^{K}n_{k}\leq n_{0}\vee\left(n/\gamma\right)^{\frac{2\beta}{2\beta+1}}

Moreover, the definition of
h
also implies
